# Neo-Nazism

This article is about Nazism after World War II. For fascist movements after World War II, see [Neo-fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism).

**Neo-Nazism** comprises all social, political, and militant ideologies and movements that have professed or idealized [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism), whether in whole or in part, since the end of [World War II](32927.md) in 1945. Neo-Nazi individuals and organizations employ their ideology to promote what they perceive as the [racial or ethnic supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supremacism) (often [White supremacy](33178.md)) of their own group; to incite or engage in [hatred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_or_racial_hatred) or [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_discrimination) against demographic minorities (often [antisemitism](1078.md) and [Islamophobia](162173.md)); and, in some cases, to establish a [fascist state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist_state) (e.g., "[Fourth Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Reich)").[^cite_note-1][^cite_note-2] Also common in neo-Nazi circles is engagement in [historical negationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_negationism) and propagation of [conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conspiracy_theories#Government,_politics,_and_conflict)—not limited to absolving or glorifying the [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party) or those who inspired or are thought to have inspired [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) and other prominent Nazi figures—such as [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) and [Jewish war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_war_conspiracy_theory); [White genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_genocide_conspiracy_theory) and [Great Replacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Replacement_conspiracy_theory); and ["cultural" Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Marxism_conspiracy_theory).&#x20;

While mainly concentrated in the [Western world](21208200.md), neo-Nazism is a global phenomenon and has organized representation in several international networks. However, it is not exclusive to [people of European origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_people)—many similar supremacist movements among local racial or ethnic groups in non-Western regions have been observed adhering to tenets of or inspired by neo-Nazi ideology. Much of this manifests in adopting original Nazi ideological doctrine and practices, including [racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_racial_theories), [ultra-nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_and_soil), [ableism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ableism#Nazi_Germany), [homophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_homosexual_men_in_Nazi_Germany), and [conspiratorial anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Bolshevism), among others.

Neo-Nazis regularly display [Nazi symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_symbolism) and express admiration for the actions of [Hitler's Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler's_Germany) before and during World War II. In a number of Western and non-Western countries (and [especially in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strafgesetzbuch_section_86a)), neo-Nazism is prohibited by laws specifically targeting Nazi ideology, in addition to general laws against hatred and discrimination. Likewise, among these countries, [Nazi-related symbols are also banned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bans_on_Nazi_symbols) as part of a long-running effort of [de-Nazification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-Nazification) that began with the [Allies of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II).[^cite_note-3]

## Definition

The term neo-Nazism describes any post–[World War II](32927.md) militant, social or political movements seeking to revive the ideology of [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) in whole or in part.[^cite_note-4][^cite_note-5]

The term 'neo-Nazism' can also refer to the ideology of these movements, which may borrow elements from Nazi doctrine, including [ultranationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalism), [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism), racism, [ableism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ableism), [xenophobia](34141.md), [homophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homophobia), [antisemitism](1078.md), up to initiating the [Fourth Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Reich). [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) is a common feature, as is the incorporation of [Nazi symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_symbolism) and admiration of [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md).

Neo-Nazism is considered a particular form of [far-right politics](221220.md) and right-wing extremism.[^cite_note-6]

### Esotericism

See also: [Esoteric neo-Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esoteric_neo-Nazism)

Neo-Nazi writers have posited a spiritual, esoteric doctrine of [race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_\(human_categorization\)), which moves beyond the primarily [Darwinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darwinian)-inspired materialist [scientific racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_racism) popular mainly in the [Anglosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglosphere) during the 20th century. Figures influential in the development of neo-Nazi racism, such as [Miguel Serrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Serrano) and [Julius Evola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Evola) (writers who are described by critics of Nazism such as the [Southern Poverty Law Center](27164.md) as influential within what it presents as parts of "the bizarre fringes of National Socialism, past and present"),[^cite_note-2015-02-08-7] claim that the [Hyperborean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperborean) ancestors of the [Aryans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryan_race) were in the distant past, far higher beings than their current state, having suffered from "involution" due to mixing with the "Telluric" peoples; supposed creations of the [Demiurge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demiurge). Within this theory, if the "Aryans" are to return to the [Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age) of the distant past, they need to awaken the memory of the blood. An [extraterrestrial](9588.md) origin of the Hyperboreans is often claimed. These theories draw influence from [Gnosticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnosticism) and [Tantrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantrism), building on the work of the [Ahnenerbe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahnenerbe). Within this racist theory, Jews are held up as the antithesis of nobility, purity and beauty.

### Ecology and environmentalism

Neo-Nazism generally aligns itself with a [blood and soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_and_soil) variation of environmentalism, which has themes in common with [deep ecology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_ecology), the [organic movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_movement) and [animal protectionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_protectionism).[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9] This tendency, sometimes called "[ecofascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecofascism)", was represented in the original German Nazism by [Richard Walther Darré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Walther_Darr%C3%A9) who was the [Reichsminister of Food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichsminister_of_Food) from 1933 until 1942.[^cite_note-10]

## History

### Germany and Austria (1945–1950s)

The final leader of the [National Socialist German Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party) (NSDAP) was [Martin Bormann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Bormann), who died on 2 May 1945 during the [Battle of Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Berlin). As his remains were not discovered until 1973, [fringe theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fringe_theories) claimed that he survived and fled to South America ([as did numerous Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratlines_\(World_War_II\))).[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-\:4-12] Similar theories [assert that Hitler faked his death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories_about_Adolf_Hitler's_death) and lived for a number of years in South America.[^cite_note-13]

The [Allied Control Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allied_Control_Council) officially dissolved the NSDAP on 10 October 1945, marking the end of "Old" Nazism. A process of [denazification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denazification) began, and the [Nuremberg trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg_trials) took place, where many major leaders and ideologues were condemned to death by October 1946, others committed suicide.



[Otto Ernst Remer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Ernst_Remer), *[Wehrmacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht)* general and leader of the postwar [Socialist Reich Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Reich_Party)

In both the East and West, surviving ex-party members and military veterans assimilated to the new reality and had no interest in constructing a "neo-Nazism". However, during the [1949 West German elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_German_federal_election,_1949) a number of Nazi advocates such as [Fritz Rössler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_R%C3%B6ssler) had infiltrated the [national conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservative) *[Deutsche Rechtspartei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Rechtspartei)*, which had five members elected. Rössler and others left to found the more radical [Socialist Reich Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Reich_Party) (SRP) under [Otto Ernst Remer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Ernst_Remer). At the onset of the [Cold War](325329.md), the SRP favoured the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) over the United States.

In [Austria](26964606.md), national independence had been restored, and the *[Verbotsgesetz 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verbotsgesetz_1947)* explicitly criminalised the NSDAP and any attempt at restoration. [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) adopted a similar law to target parties it defined as anti-constitutional; Article 21 Paragraph 2 in the [Basic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Law_for_the_Federal_Republic_of_Germany), banning the SRP in 1952 for being opposed to [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy).

As a consequence, some members of the nascent movement of German neo-Nazism joined the *Deutsche Reichspartei* of which [Hans-Ulrich Rudel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans-Ulrich_Rudel) was the most prominent figure. Younger members founded the *[Wiking-Jugend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiking-Jugend)* modelled after the [Hitler Youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler_Youth). The *Deutsche Reichspartei* stood for elections from 1953 until 1961 fetching around 1% of the vote each time. Rudel befriended French-born [Savitri Devi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savitri_Devi), who was a proponent of [Esoteric Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esoteric_Nazism). In the 1950s she wrote a number of books, such as *[Pilgrimage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilgrimage_\(book\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)* (1958), which concerns prominent [Third Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Reich) sites, and *[The Lightning and the Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lightning_and_the_Sun)* (1958), in which she claims that Hitler was an avatar of the God [Vishnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vishnu). She was not alone in this reorientation of Nazism towards its [Thulean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thule_Society)-roots; the *[Artgemeinschaft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artgemeinschaft)*, founded by former SS member Wilhelm Kusserow, attempted to promote a new [paganism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paganism).

In the [German Democratic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Democratic_Republic) (East Germany) a former member of [SA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sturmabteilung), [Wilhelm Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Adam), founded the [National Democratic Party of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_of_Germany_\(East_Germany\)). It reached out to those attracted by the Nazi Party before 1945 and provide them with a political outlet, so that they would not be tempted to support the far-right again or turn to the anti-communist Western Allies. [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) wanted to use them to create a new pro-Soviet and anti-Western strain in German politics.[^cite_note-Zubok2007-14] According to top Soviet diplomat Vladimir Semyonov, Stalin even suggested that they could be allowed to continue publishing their own newspaper, [Völkischer Beobachter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B6lkischer_Beobachter).[^cite_note-Zubok2007-14] While in Austria, former SS member Wilhelm Lang founded an esoteric group known as the [Vienna Lodge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landig_Group); he popularised [Nazism and occultism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism_and_occultism) such as the [Black Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sun_\(occult_symbol\)) and ideas of Third Reich survival colonies below the polar ice caps.



[Otto Strasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Strasser), leader of the [German Social Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Social_Union_\(West_Germany\)), returned from exile to Germany in the mid-1950s

With the onset of the [Cold War](325329.md), the allied forces had lost interest in prosecuting anyone as part of the denazification.[^cite_note-Evans-2008-15] In the mid-1950s this new political environment allowed [Otto Strasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Strasser), an NS activist on the left of the NSDAP, who had founded the [Black Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Front) to return from exile. In 1956, Strasser founded the [German Social Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Social_Union_\(West_Germany\)) as a Black Front successor, promoting a [Strasserite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strasserite) "nationalist and socialist" policy, which dissolved in 1962 due to lack of support. Other Third Reich associated groups were the [HIAG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIAG) and [Stille Hilfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stille_Hilfe) dedicated to advancing the interests of [Waffen-SS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waffen-SS) veterans and rehabilitating them into the new democratic society. However, they did not claim to be attempting to restore Nazism, instead functioning as lobbying organizations for their members before the government and the two main political parties (the conservative [CDU/CSU](10796483.md) and the Nazis' one-time archenemies, the [Social Democratic Party](60585.md))

Many bureaucrats who served under the Third Reich continued to serve in German administration after the war. According to the [Simon Wiesenthal Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Wiesenthal_Center), many of the more than 90,000 Nazi war criminals recorded in German files were serving in positions of prominence under Chancellor [Konrad Adenauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konrad_Adenauer).[^cite_note-Simon_Wiesenthal_Center-16][^cite_note-Hartmann-17] Not until the 1960s were the former concentration camp personnel prosecuted by [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) in the [Belzec trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belzec_trial), [Frankfurt Auschwitz trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurt_Auschwitz_trials), [Treblinka trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treblinka_trials), [Chełmno trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che%C5%82mno_trials), and the [Sobibór trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sobib%C3%B3r_trial).[^cite_note-Rückerl-1972-18] However, the government had passed laws prohibiting Nazis from publicly expressing their beliefs.

### "Universal National Socialism" (1950s–1970s)

| icon | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Neo-Nazism) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(May 2022)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Neo-Nazism found expression outside of Germany, including in countries who fought against the Third Reich during the Second World War, and sometimes adopted [pan-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_identity) or "universal" characteristics, beyond the parameters of [German nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_nationalism). The two main tendencies, with differing styles and even worldviews, were the followers of the American [Francis Parker Yockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Parker_Yockey), who was fundamentally [anti-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-American) and advocated for a [pan-European nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_nationalism), and those of [George Lincoln Rockwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Lincoln_Rockwell), an [American conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_conservative).[^cite_note-19]

Yockey, a neo-Spenglerian author, had written *[Imperium: The Philosophy of History and Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperium:_The_Philosophy_of_History_and_Politics)* (1949) dedicated to "the hero of the twentieth century" (namely, Adolf Hitler) and founded the [European Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Liberation_Front). He was interested more in the destiny of Europe; to this end, he advocated a [National Bolshevik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bolshevik)-esque [red-brown alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red%E2%80%93green%E2%80%93brown_alliance) against [American culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_culture) and influenced 1960s figures such as SS-veteran [Jean-François Thiriart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Fran%C3%A7ois_Thiriart). Yockey was also fond of [Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism), in particular [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser), and saw [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro)'s [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) as a positive, visiting officials there. Yockey's views impressed Otto Ernst Remer and the radical traditionalist philosopher [Julius Evola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Evola). He was constantly hounded by the [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) and was eventually arrested in 1960, before committing suicide. Domestically, Yockey's biggest sympathisers were the [National Renaissance Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Renaissance_Party_\(United_States\)), including [James H. Madole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_H._Madole), [H. Keith Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._Keith_Thompson) and [Eustace Mullins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eustace_Mullins) (*protégé* of [Ezra Pound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra_Pound)) and the [Liberty Lobby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Lobby) of [Willis Carto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willis_Carto).

Rockwell, an American conservative, was first politicised in the [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) and anti-[racial integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_integration) movements before becoming anti-Jewish. In response to his opponents calling him a "Nazi", he theatrically appropriated the aesthetic elements of the NSDAP, to "own" the intended insult. In 1959, Rockwell founded the [American Nazi Party](364176.md) and instructed his members to dress in imitation [SA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sturmabteilung)-style brown shirts, while flying the flag of the Third Reich. In contrast to Yockey, he was pro-American and cooperated with FBI requests, despite the party being targeted by [COINTELPRO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COINTELPRO) due to the mistaken belief that they were agents of Nasser's Egypt during a brief intelligence "brown scare".[^cite_note-20] Later leaders of American [white nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism) came to politics through the [American Nazi Party](364176.md) (ANP) including a teenage [David Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Duke) and [William Luther Pierce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Luther_Pierce) of the [National Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Alliance_\(United_States\)), although they soon distanced themselves from explicit self-identification with neo-Nazism.

In 1961, the [World Union of National Socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Union_of_National_Socialists) was founded by Rockwell and [Colin Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Jordan) of the British [National Socialist Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Movement_\(UK,_1962\)), adopting the *[Cotswold Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotswold_Declaration).* French socialite [Françoise Dior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7oise_Dior) was involved romantically with Jordan and his deputy [John Tyndall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Tyndall_\(politician\)) and a friend of Savitri Devi, who also attended the meeting. The National Socialist Movement wore quasi-SA uniforms, was involved in streets conflicts with the Jewish [62 Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/62_Group). In the 1970s, Tyndall's earlier involvement with neo-Nazism would come back to haunt the [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(UK\)), which he led, as they attempted to ride a wave of anti-immigration populism and concerns over British national decline. Televised exposes on *[This Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/This_Week_\(Thames_Television_TV_series\))* in 1974 and *[World in Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_in_Action)* in 1978, showed their neo-Nazi pedigree and damaged their electoral chances. In 1967, Rockwell was killed by a disgruntled former member. [Matt Koehl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Koehl) took control of the ANP, and strongly influenced by Savitri Devi, gradually transformed it into an esoteric group known as the [New Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Order_\(Neo-Nazi_group\)).

In [Franco's Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco's_Spain), certain SS refugees most notably [Otto Skorzeny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Skorzeny), [Léon Degrelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9on_Degrelle) and the son of [Klaus Barbie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klaus_Barbie) became associated with [CEDADE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEDADE) (*Círculo Español de Amigos de Europa*), an organisation which disseminated Third Reich apologetics out of [Barcelona](4443.md). They intersected with neo-Nazi advocates from [Mark Fredriksen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Fredriksen) in France to [Salvador Borrego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Borrego) in Mexico. In the post-fascist [Italian Social Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Social_Movement) splinter groups such as [Ordine Nuovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordine_Nuovo) and [Avanguardia Nazionale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avanguardia_Nazionale), involved in the "[Years of Lead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Years_of_Lead_\(Italy\))" considered Nazism a reference. [Franco Freda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco_Freda) created a "[Nazi-Maoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi-Maoism)" synthesis.

In Germany itself, the various Third Reich nostalgic movements coalesced around the [National Democratic Party of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_of_Germany) in 1964 and in Austria the [National Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_\(Austria,_1967%E2%80%9388\)) in 1967 as the primary sympathisers of the NSDAP past, although more publicly cautious than earlier groups.

### Holocaust denial and subcultures (1970s–1990s)

[Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial), the claim that [six million Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) were not deliberately and systematically exterminated as an official policy of the Third Reich and Adolf Hitler, became a more prominent feature of neo-Nazism in the 1970s. Before this time, Holocaust denial had long existed as a sentiment among neo-Nazis, but it had not yet been systematically articulated as a theory with a bibliographical canon. Few of the major theorists of Holocaust denial (who call themselves "[revisionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_revisionism)") can be uncontroversially classified as outright neo-Nazis (though some works such as those of [David Irving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Irving) forward a clearly sympathetic view of Hitler and the publisher [Ernst Zündel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Z%C3%BCndel) was deeply tied to international neo-Nazism), however, the main interest of Holocaust denial to neo-Nazis was their hope that it would help them rehabilitate their political ideology in the eyes of the general public. *[Did Six Million Really Die?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Did_Six_Million_Really_Die%3F)* (1974) by [Richard Verrall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Verrall) and *[The Hoax of the Twentieth Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hoax_of_the_Twentieth_Century)* (1976) by [Arthur Butz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Butz) are popular examples of Holocaust denial material.



The radicalisation of Flemish activist group [Vlaamse Militanten Orde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaamse_Militanten_Orde) in the 1970s energised international neo-Nazism

Key developments in international neo-Nazism during this time include the radicalisation of the *[Vlaamse Militanten Orde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaamse_Militanten_Orde)* under former [Hitler Youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler_Youth) member [Bert Eriksson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bert_Eriksson). They began hosting an annual conference; the "Iron Pilgrimage"; at [Diksmuide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diksmuide), which drew kindred ideologues from across Europe and beyond. As well as this, the [NSDAP/AO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSDAP/AO_\(1972\)) under [Gary Lauck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Lauck) arose in the United States in 1972 and challenged the international influence of the Rockwellite WUNS. Lauck's organisation drew support from the [National Socialist Movement of Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Movement_of_Denmark) of [Povl Riis-Knudsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Povl_Riis-Knudsen) and various German and Austrian figures who felt that the "National Democratic" parties were too bourgeois and insufficiently Nazi in orientation. This included [Michael Kühnen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_K%C3%BChnen), [Christian Worch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Worch), [Bela Ewald Althans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bela_Ewald_Althans) and [Gottfried Küssel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gottfried_K%C3%BCssel) of the 1977-founded [ANS/NS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_Front_of_National_Socialists/National_Activists) which called for the establishment of a Germanic [Fourth Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Reich). Some ANS/NS members were imprisoned for planning paramilitary attacks on [NATO](21133.md) bases in Germany and planning to liberate [Rudolf Hess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolf_Hess) from [Spandau Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spandau_Prison). The organisation was officially banned in 1983 by the Minister of the Interior.

During the late 1970s, a British subculture came to be associated with neo-Nazism; the [skinheads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skinheads). Portraying an ultra-masculine, crude and aggressive image, with working-class references, some of the skinheads joined the [British Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Movement) under [Michael McLaughlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_McLaughlin_\(activist\)) (successor of [Colin Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Jordan)), while others became associated with the National Front's [Rock Against Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Against_Communism) project which was meant to counter the [SWP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Workers_Party_\(UK\))'s [Rock Against Racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Against_Racism). The most significant music group involved in this project was [Skrewdriver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skrewdriver), led by [Ian Stuart Donaldson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Stuart_Donaldson). Together with ex-BM member [Nicky Crane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicky_Crane), Donaldson founded the international [Blood & Honour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_&_Honour) network in 1987. By 1992 this network, with input from [Harold Covington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Covington), had developed a paramilitary wing; [Combat 18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combat_18), which intersected with [football hooligan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_hooligan) firms such as the [Chelsea Headhunters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea_Headhunters). The neo-Nazi skinhead movement spread to the United States, with groups such as the [Hammerskins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammerskins). It was popularised from 1986 onwards by [Tom Metzger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Metzger) of the [White Aryan Resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Aryan_Resistance). Since then it has spread across the world. Films such as *[Romper Stomper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romper_Stomper)* (1992) and *[American History X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_History_X)* (1998) would fix a public perception that [neo-Nazism and skinheads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_power_skinheads) were synonymous.



Serrano identified Aryan-Hyperborean blood as the "light of the [Black Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sun_\(occult_symbol\))", a symbol found at SS-cult site [Wewelsburg Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wewelsburg)

New developments also emerged on the esoteric level, as former Chilean diplomat [Miguel Serrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Serrano) built on the works of [Carl Jung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Jung), [Otto Rahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Rahn), [Wilhelm Landig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Landig), [Julius Evola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Evola) and [Savitri Devi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savitri_Devi) to bind together and develop already existing theories. Serrano had been a member of the [National Socialist Movement of Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Movement_of_Chile) in the 1930s and from the early days of neo-Nazism, he had been in contact with key figures across Europe and beyond. Despite this, he was able to work as an ambassador to numerous countries until the rise of [Salvador Allende](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Allende). In 1984 he published his book *Adolf Hitler: The Ultimate Avatar*. Serrano claimed that the Aryans were extragalactic beings who founded [Hyperborea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperborea) and lived the heroic life of [Bodhisattvas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhisattvas), while the Jews were created by the [Demiurge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demiurge) and were concerned only with coarse [materialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materialism). Serrano claimed that a new [Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age) can be attained if the Hyperboreans repurify their blood (supposedly the light of the Black Sun) and restore their "[blood-memory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_memory_\(psychology\))". As with Savitri Devi before him, Serrano's works became a key point of reference in neo-Nazism.

### Lifting of the Iron Curtain (1990s–present)

|  | This section **may contain [original research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:No_original_research)**. Please [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Neo-Nazism\&action=edit) by [verifying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability) the claims made and adding [inline citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Citing_sources#Inline_citations). Statements consisting only of original research should be removed. *(February 2026)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

With the fall of the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall) and the [collapse of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_the_Soviet_Union) during the early 1990s, neo-Nazism began to spread its ideas in the East, as hostility to the triumphant liberal order was high and [revanchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revanchism) a widespread feeling. In Russia, during the chaos of the early 1990s, an amorphous mixture of [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) hardliners, Orthodox neo-Tsarist nostalgics (i.e., [Pamyat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamyat)) and explicit neo-Nazis found themselves strewn together in the same camp. They were united by opposition to the influence of the United States, against the liberalising legacy of [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev)'s *[perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika)* and on the [Jewish question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_question), [Soviet Zionology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Anti-Zionism) merged with a more explicit anti-Jewish sentiment. The most significant organisation representing this was [Russian National Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_National_Unity) under the leadership of [Alexander Barkashov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Barkashov), where black-uniform clad Russians marched with a red flag incorporating the [Swastika](45943.md) under the banner of *[Russia for Russians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_for_Russians).* These forces came together in a last gasp effort to save the [Supreme Soviet of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Soviet_of_Russia) against [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) during the [1993 Russian constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Russian_constitutional_crisis). As well as events in Russia, in newly independent ex-Soviet states, annual commemorations for SS volunteers now took place; particularly in [Latvia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remembrance_day_of_the_Latvian_legionnaires), [Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Waffen_Grenadier_Division_of_the_SS_\(1st_Estonian\)) and [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Waffen_Grenadier_Division_of_the_SS_\(1st_Galician\)).



Members of the [National Bolshevik Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bolshevik_Party). "Nazbols" tailor ultra-nationalist themes to a native Russian environment while still employing Nazi aesthetics.

The Russian developments excited German neo-Nazism who dreamed of a [Berlin](3354.md)–Moscow alliance against the supposedly "decadent" [Atlanticist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanticist) forces; a dream which had been thematic since the days of Remer. Zündel visited Russia and met with ex-KGB general Aleksandr Stergilov and other Russian National Unity members. Despite these initial aspirations, international neo-Nazism and its close affiliates in ultra-nationalism would be split over the [Bosnian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_War) between 1992 and 1995, as part of the [breakup of Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakup_of_Yugoslavia). The split would largely be along ethnic and sectarian lines. The Germans and the French would largely back the Western Catholic [Croats](69801.md) (Lauck's NSDAP/AO explicitly [called for volunteers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_fighters_in_the_Bosnian_War), which Kühnen's [Free German Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_German_Workers'_Party) answered and the French formed the "Groupe [Jacques Doriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Doriot)"), while the Russians and the Greeks would back the Orthodox [Serbs](59512.md) (including Russians from Barkashov's Russian National Unity, [Eduard Limonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduard_Limonov)'s [National Bolshevik Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bolshevik_Party) and [Golden Dawn](1938004.md) members joined the [Greek Volunteer Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Volunteer_Guard)). Indeed, the revival of [National Bolshevism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bolshevism) was able to steal some of the thunder from overt Russian neo-Nazism, as ultra-nationalism was wedded with veneration of [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) in place of Adolf Hitler, while still also flirting with Nazi aesthetics.

## Analogous European movements

Outside Germany, in other countries which were involved with the [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers) and had their own native ultra-nationalist movements, which sometimes collaborated with the Third Reich but were not technically German-style National Socialists, revivalist and nostalgic movements have emerged in the post-war period which, as neo-Nazism has done in Germany, seek to rehabilitate their various loosely associated ideologies. These movements include [neo-fascists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascists) and [post-fascists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-fascists) in Italy; Vichyites, Pétainists and "national Europeans" in France; [Ustaše](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e) sympathisers in [Croatia](5573.md); neo-[Chetniks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chetniks) in Serbia; [Iron Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Guard) revivalists in [Romania](25445.md); [Hungarists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarists) and [Horthyists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikl%C3%B3s_Horthy) in Hungary and others.[^cite_note-21]

## Issues

### Ex-Nazis in mainstream politics



The 1980s dispute between Austrian president [Kurt Waldheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurt_Waldheim) and the [World Jewish Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Jewish_Congress) caused an international incident

The most significant case on an international level was the election of [Kurt Waldheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurt_Waldheim) to the Presidency of Austria in 1986. It came to light that Waldheim had been a member of the [National Socialist German Students' League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_German_Students'_League), the SA and served as an intelligence officer during the Second World War. Following this he served as an Austrian diplomat and was the [Secretary-General of the United Nations](162415.md) from 1972 until 1981. After revelations of Waldheim's past were made by an Austrian journalist, Waldheim clashed with the [World Jewish Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Jewish_Congress) on the international stage. Waldheim's record was defended by [Bruno Kreisky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Kreisky), an Austrian Jew who served as Chancellor of Austria. The legacy of the affair lingers on, as [Victor Ostrovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Ostrovsky) has claimed the [Mossad](27619007.md) doctored the file of Waldheim to implicate him in war crimes.

In Finland multiple veterans of the Waffen-SS served as ministers and party chairmen.[^cite_note-Miehet-22][^cite_note-Ojanen-23][^cite_note-Westerlund-24][^cite_note-Kirjastot-25] Leader of the [National Socialist Union of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Union_of_Finland) (SKSL) [Yrjö Ruutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yrj%C3%B6_Ruutu) was a leading member of post-war [Socialist Unity Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Unity_Party_\(Finland\)).[^cite_note-KB-26] Heikki Waris, board member of SKSL served as the Minister of Social Affairs in the [Von Fieandt Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Von_Fieandt_Cabinet) in 1957.[^cite_note-27] Many more ex-Nazis were elected as members of parliament, like former SKSL [Yrjö Kilpeläinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yrj%C3%B6_Kilpel%C3%A4inen) and [Unto Varjonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unto_Varjonen)[^cite_note-KB-26] and [Torsten Aminoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torsten_Aminoff) of [People's Community Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Community_Society).[^cite_note-28]

### Contemporary right-wing populism

Some critics have sought to draw a connection between Nazism and modern [right-wing populism](8354382.md) in Europe, but the two are not widely regarded as interchangeable by most academics. In Austria, the [Freedom Party of Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Party_of_Austria) (FPÖ) served as a shelter for ex-Nazis almost from its inception.[^cite_note-Fuchs-29] In 1980, scandals undermined Austria's two main parties and the economy stagnated. [Jörg Haider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B6rg_Haider) became leader of the FPÖ and offered partial justification for [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism), calling its employment policy effective. In the [1994 Austrian election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_legislative_election,_1994), the FPÖ won 22 percent of the vote, as well as 33 percent of the vote in [Carinthia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carinthia_\(state\)) and 22 percent in Vienna; showing that it had become a force capable of reversing the old pattern of Austrian politics.[^cite_note-30]

Historian [Walter Laqueur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Laqueur) writes that even though Haider welcomed former Nazis at his meetings and went out of his way to address [Schutzstaffel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schutzstaffel) (SS) veterans, the FPÖ is not a fascist party in the traditional sense, since it has not made [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) an important issue, and it does not advocate the overthrow of the democratic order or the use of violence. In his view, the FPÖ is "not quite fascist", although it is part of a tradition, similar to that of 19th-century Viennese mayor [Karl Lueger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Lueger), which involves [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism), xenophobic populism, and authoritarianism.[^cite_note-31] Haider, who in 2005 left the Freedom Party and formed the [Alliance for Austria's Future](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_for_Austria's_Future), was killed in a traffic accident in October 2008.[^cite_note-32]

[Barbara Rosenkranz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Rosenkranz), the Freedom Party's candidate in [Austria's 2010 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Austrian_presidential_election), was controversial for having made allegedly pro-Nazi statements.[^cite_note-33] Rosenkranz is married to [Horst Rosenkranz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horst_Rosenkranz), a key member of a banned neo-Nazi party, who is known for publishing far-right books. Rosenkranz says she cannot detect anything "dishonourable" in her husband's activities.[^cite_note-34]

## Around the world

### Europe

#### Albania

[Brerore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brerore?action=edit\&redlink=1) and the [Albanian Third Position](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_Third_Position) (ATP) are neo-Nazi groups based in Albania with the ATP also having reach into [Kosovo](17391.md) and [Northern Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Macedonia).[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37] Albanian football clubs are also sometimes linked to right-wing extremism. For example *Ultras Tirona* club uses Nazi symbols in stadiums and they have unfurled banners praising the [Albanian SS Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_SS_Division). Some of the ATP's members are also members of Tirana Fanatiks football [ultra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultras) hooligan club.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39]

#### Armenia



Nzhdeh monument in Armenia

The [Armenian-Aryan Racialist Political Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosank) is a National Socialist movement in [Armenia](10918072.md). It was founded in 2021 and supports [Aryanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryanism), Antisemitism, and White supremacy.[^cite_note-40]

At least 20 cities in Armenia honor [Garegin Nzhdeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garegin_Nzhdeh) with a street or a statue or both. Nzhdeh was a Nazi collaborator who pledged the [Armenian Legion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Legion) to the service of the Third Reich.[^cite_note-41] In connection to a march in Nzhdeh's honor, [Joel Lion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joel_Lion), Israel's ambassador to Armenia stated that he was "Deeply disturbed by the march in Yerevan, on 1.1.2024, echoing 'Sieg Heil' chants. Glorifying Garegin Nzhdeh, a Nazi collaborator is unacceptable. Authorities must take a firm stand against any form of neo-nazism & antisemitism." According to [Ynet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yedioth_Ahronoth#Ynet) the Yerevan synagogue has suffered arson three times and had stones thrown through its windows in a separate incident.[^cite_note-42]

#### Belarus

There has been a Nazi presence in Belarus since at least 1933 in the form of the [Belarusian National Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarusian_National_Socialist_Party?action=edit\&redlink=1). Neo-Nazi White Legion (Белы Легіён) attempted a bombing of a Soviet Victory Monument in Minsk.[^cite_note-Kotljarchuk-43] In 2020, Dynamo Minsk fans unfurled a banner during a match with a picture of [Rudolf Hess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolf_Hess) and the text "For us, your life is an iconic example of loyalty". White Power Skinhead groups include "White Willpower" (Белая Воля, *Byelaya Volya*) and Support88. In 2004 a magazine by the name of the Belarusian Resistance (Беларускі Рэзыстанс, *Byelaruski Rezystans*) with editor-in-chief Siarhej Iorsh was first published, with the focus of the magazine being rehabilitating the Belarusians who fought the Red Army.[^cite_note-Kotljarchuk-43][^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45]

Since the independence of Belarus, the far-right in Belarus has systematically rehabilitated [Belarusian nazi collaborators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byelorussian_collaboration_with_Nazi_Germany) both in the internet and real life. For example in 2014, the right-wing organization Young Front demonstrated with banners depicting General [Michał Vituška](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micha%C5%82_Vitu%C5%A1ka), nazi collaborator and anti-Soviet partisan. In 2018, the biggest alcohol company in the country Bulbash United posted a picture of General [Francišak Kušal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franci%C5%A1ak_Ku%C5%A1al), a prominent Nazi collaborator, and a text praising him attached to it on their webpage. It drew both condemnation and praise from the netizens of Belarus. Aliaksei Dzermant is the founder of Kryuskaja Draugija Druvingau, Belarusian branch of the neo-Nazi pagan [Allgermanische Heidnische Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allgermanische_Heidnische_Front). Dzermant is also the founder of the modern successor of the Belarusian Nazi Party.[^cite_note-Kotljarchuk-43]

#### Belgium

Main article: [Bloed, Bodem, Eer en Trouw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloed,_Bodem,_Eer_en_Trouw)

A Belgian neo-Nazi organization, [Bloed, Bodem, Eer en Trouw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloed,_Bodem,_Eer_en_Trouw) (Blood, Soil, Honour and Loyalty), was created in 2004 after splitting from the international network ([Blood and Honour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_and_Honour)). The group rose to public prominence in September 2006, after 17 members were arrested under the December 2003 [anti-terrorist laws](4700071.md) and laws against racism, [antisemitism](1078.md) and supporters of censorship. According to Justice Minister [Laurette Onkelinx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurette_Onkelinx) and Interior Minister [Patrick Dewael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Dewael), the suspects (11 of whom were members of the military) were preparing to launch terrorist attacks in order to "destabilize" [Belgium](3343.md).[^cite_note-46] According to the journalist [Manuel Abramowicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Abramowicz), of the Resistances,[^cite_note-47] the extremists of the radical right have always had as its aim to "infiltrate the state mechanisms", including the army in the 1970s and the 1980s, through [Westland New Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westland_New_Post) and the [Front de la Jeunesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_de_la_Jeunesse_\(Belgium\)).[^cite_note-48]

A police operation, which mobilized 150 agents, searched five military barracks (in [Leopoldsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopoldsburg) near the Dutch border, Kleine-Brogel, [Peer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer,_Belgium), Brussels (Royal military school) and [Zedelgem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zedelgem)) as well as 18 private addresses in [Flanders](10878.md). They found weapons, munitions, explosives and a homemade bomb large enough to make "a car explode". The leading suspect, B.T., was organizing the trafficking of weapons and was developing international links, in particular with the Dutch far-right movement [De Nationale Alliantie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Alliance_\(Netherlands\)).[^cite_note-49]

#### Bosnia and Herzegovina

The neo-Nazi [white nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism) organization Bosanski Pokret Nacionalnog Ponosa ([Bosnian Movement of National Pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_Movement_of_National_Pride)) was founded in [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md) in July 2009. Its model is the [Waffen-SS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waffen-SS) [Handschar Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/13th_Waffen_Mountain_Division_of_the_SS_Handschar_\(1st_Croatian\)), which was composed of [Bosniak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosniaks) volunteers.[^cite_note-50] It proclaimed its main enemies to be "Jews, [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people), Serbian [Chetniks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chetniks), the [Croatian separatists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Republic_of_Herzeg-Bosnia), [Josip Broz Tito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josip_Broz_Tito), [Communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism), homosexuals and [blacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people)".[^cite_note-51] Its ideology is a mixture of [Bosnian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_nationalism), [National Socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) and [white nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism). It says "Ideologies that are not welcome in Bosnia are: Zionism, Islamism, communism, capitalism. The only ideology good for us is Bosnian nationalism because it secures national prosperity and social justice..."[^cite_note-52] The group is led by a person nicknamed Sauberzwig, after the commander of the 13th SS Handschar. The group's strongest area of operations is in the Tuzla area of Bosnia.

#### Bulgaria

The primary neo-Nazi political party to receive attention in post-WWII Bulgaria is the [Bulgarian National Union – New Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_National_Union_%E2%80%93_New_Democracy).

On 13 February of every year since 2003, Bulgarian neo-Nazis and like-minded far-right nationalists gather at [Sofia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofia,_Bulgaria) to honor [Hristo Lukov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hristo_Lukov), a late World War II general known for his antisemitic and pro-Nazi stance. From 2003 to 2019, the annual event was hosted by Bulgarian National Union.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55]

Bulgaria is also home to a neo-Nazi group called the White Front that is "linked to an extremely violent fringe of neo-Nazis" that have defaced synagogues with antisemitic posters. White Front also countered Sofia Pride by plastering around homophobic posters claiming homosexuality is connected to pedophilia.[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57]

On August 5, 2026, a man was killed in a livestreamed lynching by a gang of teenage neo-Nazis and the incident sparked discussion of neo-Nazi vigilantism in Bulgaria.[^cite_note-58] Later in August 2026, a crowd of Bulgarian neo-Nazis sieged a hotel hosting Jewish tourists, attempting to break the door and windows. The neo-Nazis chanted "Sieg heil" and performed Nazi salutes and left only when dispersed by the police.[^cite_note-59]

[Valeri Simeonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valeri_Simeonov), former deputy Prime Minister, has been described as employing neo-Nazi rhetoric. Simeonov characterized Romanis as "feral humanoids" and described giving them equal rights as a "sinister experiment" and "Bolshevism" and talked of the need for concentration camps in Bulgaria. Simeonov was later appointed to co-chair the National Council on Co-operation on Ethnic and Integration Issues.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61]

#### Croatia

See also: [Far-right politics in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Croatia)



Young boy wearing a shirt with a [Black Legion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Legion_\(Usta%C5%A1e_militia\)) sign at a [Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thompson_\(band\)) concert



Graffiti depicting the U symbol of the [Ustashe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ustashe) during the [Anti-Cyrillic protests in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Cyrillic_protests_in_Croatia)

Neo-Nazis in [Croatia](5573.md) base their ideology on the writings of [Ante Pavelić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ante_Paveli%C4%87) and the [Ustaše](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e), a [fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism) anti-Yugoslav separatist movement.[^cite_note-62] The Ustaše regime committed a [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocides_in_history_\(World_War_I_through_World_War_II\)#Serbs_in_the_Independent_State_of_Croatia) against [Serbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_persecution_of_Serbs), [Jews and Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_the_Independent_State_of_Croatia). At the end of [World War II](32927.md), many Ustaše members fled to the West, where they found sanctuary and continued their political and [terrorist](30636.md) activities (which were tolerated due to [Cold War](325329.md) hostilities).[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64]

In 1999, Zagreb's [Square of the Victims of Fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_of_the_Victims_of_Fascism) was renamed *Croatian Nobles Square*, provoking widespread criticism of Croatia's attitude towards the [Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust).[^cite_note-65] In 2000, the [Zagreb City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagreb_City_Council) again renamed the square into *Square of the Victims of Fascism*.[^cite_note-66] Many streets in Croatia were renamed after the prominent Ustaše figure [Mile Budak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mile_Budak), which provoked outrage amongst the Serbian minority. Since 2002, there has been a reversal of this development, and streets with the name of Mile Budak or other persons connected with the Ustaše movement are few or non-existent.[^cite_note-news.bbc.co.uk-67] A plaque in [Slunj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slunj) with the inscription "Croatian Knight [Jure Francetić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jure_Franceti%C4%87)" was erected to commemorate Francetić, the notorious Ustaše leader of the Black Legion. The plaque remained there for four years, until it was removed by the authorities.[^cite_note-news.bbc.co.uk-67][^cite_note-68]

In 2003, Croatian [penal code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penal_code) was amended with provisions prohibiting the public display of Nazi symbols, the propagation of Nazi ideology, [historical revisionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_revisionism) and [holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) but the amendments were annulled in 2004 since they were not enacted in accordance with a constitutionally prescribed procedure.[^cite_note-69] Nevertheless, since 2006 Croatian penal code explicitly prohibits any type of [hate crime](13547.md) based on [race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_\(classification_of_human_beings\)), [color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_skin_color), [gender](38076.md), [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation), religion or national origin.[^cite_note-70]

There have been instances of [hate speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech) in Croatia, such as the use of the phrase *[Srbe na vrbe!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srbe_na_vrbe!)* ("\[Hang] Serbs on the [willow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willow) trees!"). In 2004, an [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_Croatia) church was [spray-painted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spray_painting) with pro-Ustaše graffiti.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] During some protests in Croatia, supporters of [Ante Gotovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ante_Gotovina) and other at the time suspected [war criminals](48626.md) (all acquitted in 2012) have carried [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) symbols and pictures of Pavelić.[^cite_note-73] On 17 May 2007, a concert in Zagreb by [Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thompson_\(band\)), a popular Croatian singer, was attended by 60,000 people, some of them wearing Ustaše uniforms. Some gave Ustaše salutes and shouted the Ustaše slogan "*[Za dom spremni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Za_dom_spremni)*" ("For the homeland – ready!"). This event prompted the [Simon Wiesenthal Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Wiesenthal_Center) to publicly issue a protest to the Croatian president.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78] Cases of displaying Ustashe memorabilia have been recorded at the [Bleiburg commemoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bleiburg_commemoration) held annually in Austria.[^cite_note-79]

#### Cyprus

The neo-Nazi [ELAM](31470503.md) party, inspired by the Greek Golden Dawn, is the third biggest party and has 10% of the seats in the [House of Representatives](4497460.md) and a representative in the European Parliament. [^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82]

#### Czech Republic

The neo-Nazi [Workers' Party of Social Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_Party_of_Social_Justice) (DSSS) was shut down because a court found that it tried to replace democracy with a "National Socialist system", which is illegal in the Czech Republic. Former DSSS leaders [Tomáš Vandas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1%C5%A1_Vandas) and Jiří Štěpánek continue their political activities in “Bezpečné ulice” (Safe Streets) political initiative. In 2011 Vandas was found guilty of defaming minorities but was granted amnesty by the Czech President [Václav Klaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%A1clav_Klaus). Predecessor of the DSSS, [Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_Party_\(Czech_Republic\)) (DS) sent party members and skinheads to patrol Romani ghettoes, and these patrols were joined by non-Romani neighbors, along with the DS youth organization Dělnická mládež, and extremist groups such as the National Party and National Resistance. These patrols led to anti-Romani pogroms in multiple cities, and molotov cocktails were thrown into Romani apartments, most famously in the [2009 Vítkov arson attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_V%C3%ADtkov_arson_attack).[^cite_note-83]

Numerous Romani houses were burned down in the [2013 Czech Anti-Roma protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Czech_Anti-Roma_protests) that were supported by Czech neo-Nazi groups.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85] In [České Budějovice](149384.md), for example, hundreds of neo-Nazis shouted "Heil Hitler" and gave nazi salutes as they rampaged through a Romani neighborhood, torching houses.[^cite_note-86]

In 2021 a nine foot memorial for the Nazi collaborationist [Russian Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Liberation_Army) (ROA) was erected in Prague. The commander of the ROA General [Andrey Vlasov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrey_Vlasov) also has a memorial in Lnáře Castle in [Lnáře](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ln%C3%A1%C5%99e).[^cite_note-87]

In mid-September 2025, an anti-racist demonstration in [Frýdek-Místek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fr%C3%BDdek-M%C3%ADstek) was attacked by 30 armed neo-Nazis, leaving multiple demonstrators injured, one in critical condition.[^cite_note-88]

The Czech MP [Filip Turek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filip_Turek) has been accused of "adoring" Nazis by the Czech president [Petr Pavel](42272533.md). Turek has, among other things, said that it should be considered a mitigating factor that the victims of the Vítkov arson attack were Romani. Turek was pictured doing a nazi salute, collects Nazi memorabilia, and supports the neo-Nazi [Golden Dawn](1938004.md). He also called the [Christchurch mosque shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christchurch_mosque_shooting) a "cleaning up of New Zealand", called Obama a "nigger" and stated "White men built and, thank God, continue to build this world as we know it. Is there really a retard who finds this unnatural?" Turek was proposed to be the Minister of Foreign Affairs in the Babis coalition.[^cite_note-89]

#### Denmark

The [National Socialist Movement of Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Movement_of_Denmark) was formed in 1991, and was formally a neo-Nazi party, that would actively promote the Nazi ideology in Denmark. The party did not gain any political influence, and were regarded as a failed political project by neo-Nazi expert Frede Farmand.[^cite_note-90] Long time party leader Johnni Hansen was replaced by Esben Rohde Kristensen in 2010, which resulted in a large amount of party members leaving the party. While the party never has been formally dissolved, there has been very little activity from its core member since 2010.[^cite_note-91] Former neo-Nazi [Daniel Carlsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Carlsen?action=edit\&redlink=1) formed the small national party [Party of the Danes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_the_Danes) in 2011, which officially rejected Nazism, but were none the less categorized as such by professor in politics Peter Nedergaard.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] It was dissolved in 2017 after its founder [Daniel Stockholm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Stockholm) announced retirement from politics.[^cite_note-94]

#### Estonia

[Standartenführer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standartenf%C3%BChrer) (Colonel) in the SS and recipient of the [Knight's Cross of the Iron Cross with Oak Leaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knight's_Cross_of_the_Iron_Cross_with_Oak_Leaves) [Alfons Rebane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfons_Rebane) was reburied in Estonia with full military honors in 1999. In 2004 a memorial to Rebane was unveiled in northern Estonia and the event was attended by members of parliament. Russia's chief rabbi, [Berel Lazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berel_Lazar) condemned the event as serving to escalate neo-Nazism and anti-Semitism.[^cite_note-95] In 2018 a memorial for Rebane was installed in [Mustla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustla) where he lived.[^cite_note-96]

In 2006, Roman Ilin, a Jewish theatre director from [St. Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Petersburg), Russia, was attacked by neo-Nazis when returning from a tunnel after a rehearsal. Ilin subsequently accused Estonian police of indifference after filing the incident.[^cite_note-97] When a dark-skinned French student was attacked in [Tartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartu), the head of an association of foreign students claimed that the attack was characteristic of a wave of neo-Nazi violence. An Estonian police official, however, stated that there were only a few cases involving foreign students over the previous two years.[^cite_note-98] In November 2006, the Estonian government passed a law banning the display of [Nazi symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_symbolism).[^cite_note-99]

The 2008 [United Nations Human Rights Council](635790.md) Special Rapporteur's Report noted that community representatives and non-governmental organizations devoted to human rights had pointed out that neo-Nazi groups were active in Estonia—particularly in Tartu—and had perpetrated acts of violence against non-European minorities.[^cite_note-100]

A leading figure in the former neo-Nazi [Estonian Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Independence_Party) (EIP) [Risto Teinonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risto_Teinonen) was allegedly organizing neo-Nazi military training camps in Estonia. Teinonen is an open neo-Nazi, he has organized parties on the anniversary of the [Wannsee Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wannsee_Conference) and on Hitler's birthday where he has appeared in a Nazi uniform. Teinonen was also a member of a [political clique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bolshevism#Estonia) funded by the antisemitic nationalist organization [National Patriotic Front "Memory"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Patriotic_Front_%22Memory%22).[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102][^cite_note-backman+teinonen-103][^cite_note-104] Teinonen's Finnish associate [Johan Bäckman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johan_B%C3%A4ckman) ([VKK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_Belongs_to_the_People)) has also been accused of recruiting far-right individuals that have gone on to take part in the military camps of the neo-Nazi [Russian Imperial Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Imperial_Movement) (RIM).[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108] RIM also has a training club in Estonia. Both Bäckman and ethnic Russian associates of the RIM were deported from Estonia as [persona non gratas](521241.md).[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-banned-110]

The neo-Nazi terrorist organization [Feuerkrieg Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feuerkrieg_Division) was found and operates in the country, with some members of the [Conservative People's Party of Estonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_People's_Party_of_Estonia) (EKRE) having been linked to the Feuerkrieg Division.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] Estonia also has an [Active Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_Club) chapter that was allegedly founded with the support of Estonian Atomwaffen member. In 2024, three Active Club Estonia members were convicted of far-right vigilantism. According to an [Estonian Internal Security Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estonian_Internal_Security_Service) report Active Club Estonia is "a group mainly engaged in promoting [Tesak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesak)-style vigilante operations in Estonia".[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] In January 2025, [Harju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harju_County) court found Feuerkrieg Division to be a terrorist organization and several members were sentenced to prison terms.[^cite_note-Harju-117] [Atomwaffen Division Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomwaffen_Division_Finland) maintained particularly close relations with the Feuerkrieg Division in Estonia.[^cite_note-118] Feuerkrieg also co-operated with the Eastern European terrorist group [Maniac Murder Cult](78379983.md).[^cite_note-119]

As of 2026, former EIP chairman [Sven Kivisildnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sven_Kivisildnik) serves in [Pärnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A4rnu) City Council on the EKRE list. Valmar Veste, also EKRE, is slated to become the mayor of Pärnu in 2026. Veste is known for operating a company importing and selling [Thor Steinar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thor_Steinar) brand clothing, considered closely associated with neo-Nazism by the *[Verfassungschutz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution)*.[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123]

In 2024 an Estonian neo-Nazi and a member of Estonian chapter of neo-Nazi [Russian National Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_National_Unity) Allan Hantsom was convicted of vandalizing the car of the Interior Minister [Lauri Laanemets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauri_Laanemets).[^cite_note-124]

#### Finland

Further information: [Far-right politics in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Finland)



[Pekka Siitoin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekka_Siitoin), Finnish neo-Nazi, [occultist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occultist), and [Satanist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satanism)

[^cite_note-125]

[^cite_note-126]



Finns Party Minister of Economic Affairs [Vilhelm Junnila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilhelm_Junnila) caused controversy for speaking at a [White Lives Matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Lives_Matter) demonstration in [Turku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku), described as a neo-Nazi event

In Finland, neo-Nazism is often connected to the 1930s and 1940s fascist and pro-Nazi [Patriotic People's Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_People's_Movement) (IKL), its youth movement [Blues-and-Blacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blues-and-Blacks) and its predecessor [Lapua Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapua_Movement). Post-war fascist groups such as [Patriotic People's Movement (1993)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_People's_Movement_\(1993\)), [Patriotic Popular Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_Popular_Front), [Patriotic National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_Independence), [Blue-and-Black Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue-and-Black_Movement) and many others consciously copy the style of the movement and look up to its leaders as inspiration. A [Finns Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finns_Party) councillor and police officer in Seinäjoki caused controversy by wearing the fascist blue-and-black uniform.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

During the Cold War, all partied deemed fascist were banned according to the [Paris Peace Treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Treaties) and all former fascist activists had to find new political homes.[^cite_note-130] Despite [Finlandization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finlandization), many continued in public life. Three former members of the Waffen SS served as ministers; the [Finnish SS Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_SS_Battalion) officers [Sulo Suorttanen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulo_Suorttanen) ([Centre Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Party_\(Finland\))) and [Pekka Malinen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekka_Malinen) ([People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Finland_\(1951\))) as well as [Mikko Laaksonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikko_Laaksonen) ([Social Democrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Finland)), a soldier in the [Finnish SS-Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_volunteers_in_the_Waffen-SS#Finnish_SS-Company), formed of pro-Nazi defectors.[^cite_note-Westerlund-24][^cite_note-Kirjastot-25] Chairman of the [Constitutional Right Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Right_Party) Ilpo Järvinen was likewise an SS-Company veteran.[^cite_note-Miehet-22][^cite_note-Ojanen-23]

Neo-Nazi activism was limited to small illegal groups like the [clandestine Nazi occultist group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turku_Society_for_the_Spiritual_Sciences) led by [Pekka Siitoin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pekka_Siitoin) who made headlines after [arson and bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kursiivi_printing_house_arson) of the printing houses of the [Communist Party of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Finland). His associates also sent [letter bombs](49760.md) to leftists, including to the headquarters of the [Finnish Democratic Youth League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left_Youth_\(Finland\)).[^cite_note-131] Another group called the "New Patriotic People's Movement" bombed the left-wing *[Kansan Uutiset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansan_Uutiset)* newspaper and the embassy of communist Bulgaria.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134]

Member of the [Nordic Realm Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Realm_Party) Seppo Seluska was convicted of the torture and murder of a gay Jewish person.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-\:1-136][^cite_note-137]

The skinhead culture gained momentum during the late 1980s and peaked during the late 1990s. In 1991, Finland received a number of Somali immigrants who became the main target of Finnish skinhead violence in the following years, including four attacks using explosives and a racist murder. Asylum seeker centres were attacked, in [Joensuu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joensuu) skinheads would force their way into an asylum seeker centre and start shooting with shotguns. At worst Somalis were assaulted by 50 skinheads at the same time.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139]

The most prominent neo-Nazi group is the [Nordic Resistance Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Resistance_Movement), which is tied to multiple murders, attempted murders and assaults of political enemies was found in 2006 and proscribed in 2019.[^cite_note-140] The second biggest Finnish party, the [Finns Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finns_Party) politicians have frequently supported far-right and neo-Nazi movements such as the Finnish Defense League, Soldiers of Odin, Nordic Resistance Movement, Rajat Kiinni (Close the Borders), and Suomi Ensin (Finland First).[^cite_note-141] In the 1990s and 2000s, before the breakthrough of the Finns Party, a few neo-Nazi candidates enjoyed success, like Janne Kujala of [Finland - Fatherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland_-_Fatherland) (founded as Aryan Germanic Brotherhood) and [Jouni Lanamäki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jouni_Lanam%C3%A4ki) who was previously associated with the [Nordic Reich Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_Reich_Party).[^cite_note-hs2017-142][^cite_note-143] Pekka Siitoin of the [National Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_\(Finland\)) was the fifth most popular candidate in [Naantali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naantali) city council elections.[^cite_note-144]

The NRM, Finns party and other far-right nationalist parties organize an [annual torch march demonstration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/612_march) in Helsinki in memory of the Finnish SS-battalion on the [Finnish independence day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Finland\)) which ends at the [Hietaniemi cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hietaniemi_cemetery) where members visit the tomb of [Carl Gustaf Emil Mannerheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Gustaf_Emil_Mannerheim) and the monument to the Finnish SS Battalion.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146] The event is protested by antifascists, leading to counterdemonstrators being violently assaulted by NRM members who act as security. The demonstration attracts close to 3,000 participants according to the estimates of the police and hundreds of officers patrol Helsinki to prevent violent clashes.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148][^cite_note-holappa-149][^cite_note-150]

#### France

Main article: [History of far-right movements in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_far-right_movements_in_France)



French neo-fascist groups adopted the [Celtic cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_cross) as an ambiguous "Christian and pagan" symbol in the 1940s

In France, the most enthusiastic collaborationists during the [German occupation of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_military_administration_in_occupied_France_during_World_War_II) had been the [National Popular Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Popular_Rally) of [Marcel Déat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_D%C3%A9at) (former [SFIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Section_of_the_Workers'_International) members) and the [French Popular Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Popular_Party) of [Jacques Doriot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Doriot) (former [French Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Communist_Party) members). These two groups, like the Germans, saw themselves as combining ultra-nationalism and [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism). In the south there existed the vassal state of [Vichy France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vichy_France) under the military "Hero of the Verdun", Marshal [Philippe Pétain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_P%C3%A9tain) whose *[Révolution nationale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9volution_nationale)* emphasised an authoritarian Catholic conservative politics. Following the [liberation of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_of_France) and the creation of the [Fourth French Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_French_Republic), collaborators were prosecuted during the *[épuration légale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89puration_l%C3%A9gale)* and nearly 800 put to death for treason under [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle).

In the aftermath of the Second World War, the main concern of the French radical right was the collapse of the [French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonial_empire), in particular the [Algerian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_War), which led to the creation of the [OAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_arm%C3%A9e_secr%C3%A8te). Outside of this, individual fascistic activists such as [Maurice Bardèche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Bard%C3%A8che) (brother-in-law of [Robert Brasillach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Brasillach)), as well as SS-veterans [Saint-Loup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Augier) and [René Binet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Binet_\(neo-Fascist\)), were active in France and involved in the [European Social Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Social_Movement) and later the [New European Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_European_Order), alongside similar groups from across Europe. Early neo-fascist groups included [Jeune Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeune_Nation), which introduced the [Celtic cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtic_cross) into use by radical right groups (an association which would spread internationally). A "neither East, nor West" pan-Europeanism was most popular among French fascistic activists until the late 1960s, partly motivated by feelings of national vulnerability following the collapse of their empire; thus the Belgian SS-veteran [Jean-François Thiriart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Fran%C3%A7ois_Thiriart)'s group [Jeune Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeune_Europe) also had a considerable French contingent.
